He timed their passage against the peaks and estimated a speed of about 1,700 km/h, far above any known aircraft of the era. His description of the motion —'like a saucer skipped across water'— led the press to coin the term 'flying saucer.' It is the foundational sighting of the modern era of the phenomenon: the one that triggered the 1947 wave and the vocabulary we still use. Later prosaic explanations (mirages, meteors, birds) never closed it conclusively."}]]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"hidden w-16 text-right font-mono text-xs tabular-nums text-muted sm:inline","children":"1947"}],["$","span",null,{"className":"w-12 shrink-0 text-right font-mono text-xs text-tierA","title":"Nivel de evidencia · S sólido · A aceptable · B folklórico","children":"A"}],["$","span",null,{"className":"w-12 shrink-0 text-right font-mono text-sm font-semibold tabular-nums text-text","title":"Probabilidad del caso · qué tan probable es un fenómeno genuinamente no explicado (distinta de la partición de explicaciones)","children":[52,"%"]}]]}]}]

6a:T45d,Document 341_110677 (PURSUE Release 01) contains Air Intelligence Information Report IR 193-55, signed on October 14, 1955 by U.S. air attaché Lt. Col. Thomas S. Ryan. It records the account of three 'highly reliable' U.S. observers who, traveling by rail through the Trans-Caucasus (between Aliaty and Adzhijabul, south of Baku) on October 4, 1955 at 19:10, saw two disc-shaped objects ascend almost vertically —with the outer surface revolving slowly to the right— to about 6,000 feet, at which point they sharply accelerated northward. The take-off area, 1-2 miles south of the line, showed two searchlight beams pointing almost vertically. The attaché assessed it as significant for the USAF's UFO project. What the document doesn't say —and a separate CIA memo (CIA-UAP-D020, declassified in PURSUE Release 04) does reveal— is the identity of those 'three observers': Senator Richard Russell, then chairman of the Senate Armed Services Committee; his military aide, the same Lt. Col. E. U. Hathaway cited as the report's source; and interpreter Ruben Efron. 1a2:T6cb,PURSUE (Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters) is the executive directive signed by the Trump administration in May 2026 ordering the newly renamed Department of War (formerly DoD) to execute an 'expeditious' interagency release of unresolved UAP records. The war.gov/ufo/ portal opened on May 8, 2026 and published in successive tranches: Release 1 (May 8), Release 2 (May 22), Release 3 (June 12), Release 4 (July 10) and Release 5 (August 7). By mid-June the portal had surpassed 1.7 billion accesses. The AARO assessment accompanying the third release (signed by its director Jon Kosloski, June 5) emphasizes 'plasma-like' orbs —including an orange 'mother orb' releasing smaller red orbs— and concedes that ~40% of cases remain unexplained. The fourth release (July 10, 2026) contributed 40 additional files (14 documents, 19 videos, 4 audio recordings, 3 images), including infrared video of a six-pointed object over the Yellow Sea captured by U.S. Indo-Pacific Command in 2025, testimony from a military aviator with 28 years of service about a rectangular object with 'flight characteristics unlike anything I had seen' (Eastern US, 2019), documentation of an intrusion over the Pantex nuclear plant (Texas, September 2015), and a transcript of a Los Alamos conference (1949) where physicists attempted to explain the 'green fireballs' over the nuclear laboratory. It is the first U.S. federal UAP-release infrastructure with a direct presidential mandate and a stable public URL. The content remains more procedural than probative —orb videos, historical audio and declassified documents, without the conclusive piece that would settle the material question— but it is no longer an empty
